========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I came in because my head’s been… foggy, I guess is the word. I work around cataloguing notes, old ones mostly, and I used to be able to keep my place just fine—like, you know, the little numbers and entries, they have a rhythm to them after a while. Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.

And then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. It’s frustrating.

My appetite, yes… that’s been different. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t eaten unless I sort of remind myself.

I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. Lately I mostly just sit at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of… faded. Like the color’s gone out of things. Even opening those books makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three months now, most days. It sort of crept in around the time I started getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through, old forms and stacks of notes, and I kept thinking I should be feeling something different about it, but mostly I just felt foggy and low. So, yeah… around three months.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. There was one with this little sketch of a bridge tucked in the back once, and I remember wondering who had owned it before me… but lately I just end up sitting at the kitchen table with them there, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes when I’m sitting there and it’s quiet, I think about how strange it is that people leave all these little traces behind—notes in books, old checkout cards, handwriting—and then they’re just gone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really… I don’t have something clear to tell you about that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: My appetite, yes… that’s been different. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t eaten unless I sort of remind myself.\n\nMy wife notices it more than I do, I think. She’ll say, “You barely touched that,” because I leave the rice and soup half-finished. And I never used to waste a meal. My mother was very strict about that, actually—she’d save even the smallest bit of broth for the next day. But lately I just sort of sit there, and the bowl gets cold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not like I’m limping or anything, but my whole body feels slow. Even walking to the corner store feels like it takes more out of me than it should.\n\nAt work, too, just sorting papers on my desk… I used to do that without thinking, you know, make little piles, straighten things up. Now I sit there looking at them, and it feels like my hands take a long time to get started. Funny, my father used to say a cluttered desk meant a cluttered mind, though his garage was a disaster… but yes, I guess I do feel slower.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head’s been… foggy, I guess is the word. I work around cataloguing notes, old ones mostly, and I used to be able to keep my place just fine—like, you know, the little numbers and entries, they have a rhythm to them after a while. Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it. I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head’s been… foggy, I guess is the word. I work around cataloguing notes, old ones mostly, and I used to be able to keep my place just fine—like, you know, the little numbers and entries, they have a rhythm to them after a while. Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it. I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. Lately I mostly just sit at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of… faded. Like the color’s gone out of things. Even opening those books makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three months now, most days. It sort of crept in around the time I started getting ready to leave the library. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head’s been… foggy, I guess is the word. I work around cataloguing notes, old ones mostly, and I used to be able to keep my place just fine—like, you know, the little numbers and entries, they have a rhythm to them after a while. Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it. I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. Lately I mostly just sit at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of… faded. Like the color’s gone out of things. Even opening those books makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three months now, most days. It sort of crept in around the time I started getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through, old forms and stacks of notes, and I kept thinking I should be feeling something different about it, but mostly I just felt foggy and low. So, yeah… around three months.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. There was one with this little sketch of a bridge tucked in the back once, and I remember wondering who had owned it before me… but lately I just end up sitting at the kitchen table with them there, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe tiny moments, but not really pleasure. I’ll sit at the kitchen table most days, and I keep thinking I should look at my old watercolor books—there’s one with seaside pictures, and it used to remind me of this little print my aunt had, funny how you remember things like that.

But lately when I open it, it doesn’t feel nice. It just feels… heavy. Like the color’s gone out of everything, even the things that used to have color. So no, I can’t really say I enjoy much.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head’s been… foggy, I guess is the word. I work around cataloguing notes, old ones mostly, and I used to be able to keep my place just fine—like, you know, the little numbers and entries, they have a rhythm to them after a while. Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it. I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. Lately I mostly just sit at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of… faded. Like the color’s gone out of things. Even opening those books makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three months now, most days. It sort of crept in around the time I started getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through, old forms and stacks of notes, and I kept thinking I should be feeling something different about it, but mostly I just felt foggy and low. So, yeah… around three months.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. There was one with this little sketch of a bridge tucked in the back once, and I remember wondering who had owned it before me… but lately I just end up sitting at the kitchen table with them there, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes when I’m sitting there and it’s quiet, I think about how strange it is that people leave all these little traces behind—notes in books, old checkout cards, handwriting—and then they’re just gone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really… I don’t have something clear to tell you about that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: My appetite, yes… that’s been different. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t eaten unless I sort of remind myself.\n\nMy wife notices it more than I do, I think. She’ll say, “You barely touched that,” because I leave the rice and soup half-finished. And I never used to waste a meal. My mother was very strict about that, actually—she’d save even the smallest bit of broth for the next day. But lately I just sort of sit there, and the bowl gets cold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not like I’m limping or anything, but my whole body feels slow. Even walking to the corner store feels like it takes more out of me than it should.\n\nAt work, too, just sorting papers on my desk… I used to do that without thinking, you know, make little piles, straighten things up. Now I sit there looking at them, and it feels like my hands take a long time to get started. Funny, my father used to say a cluttered desk meant a cluttered mind, though his garage was a disaster… but yes, I guess I do feel slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I can’t say a number. But I suppose I might have, because I’m eating less. I have to remind myself sometimes, like, “Oh, you haven’t had lunch,” which is odd.\n\nMy wife notices more than I do. She’ll point out that I left half the rice or soup, and I never used to do that. Wasting food was a big thing in our house growing up… you didn’t leave rice in the bowl. So, yes, maybe, but I don’t know exactly how much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently difficult?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty consistently difficult, I’d say. I mean, there are little moments where I get distracted and it doesn’t feel as heavy, but most days… the low mood and that foggy feeling have been there.\n\nIt’s been about three months now, I think. Around when I started getting things ready to leave the library. Which is strange, because you’d think having time away from all those files and carts and little date stamps would feel like a relief, but it hasn’t really worked that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two weeks where you felt even slightly better for at least a couple of days?\n\nPatient: Not really for a couple of days, no. I mean, there might be a half hour where I’m watching the birds out by the fence and I forget myself a little, but mostly… most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nEven my old watercolor books, the ones I used to like looking through—there’s one with these seaside scenes, my aunt had something similar in her hallway years ago—now I open them and instead of feeling interested, I just feel heavy. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a couple of better days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt normal, like your old self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve really felt like my old self. Most days I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nEven those old watercolor books I used to enjoy—there’s one with these soft seaside paintings, I used to look at it for ages—now I open it and it just makes me feel heavy instead of interested. So, no, not normal, really.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I keep thinking maybe I’ll get some comfort from the things I used to like… like those old watercolor books I’ve got. There’s one with these washed-out seaside pictures, and I used to sit with it a long while, noticing little bits of color.\n\nBut lately I just end up at the kitchen table, and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it all. I open the book and instead of enjoying it, it just makes me feel heavy. So I can’t really say there’s anything I’m looking forward to much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still enjoy or find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: Maybe tiny moments, but not really pleasure. I’ll sit at the kitchen table most days, and I keep thinking I should look at my old watercolor books—there’s one with seaside pictures, and it used to remind me of this little print my aunt had, funny how you remember things like that.\n\nBut lately when I open it, it doesn’t feel nice. It just feels… heavy. Like the color’s gone out of everything, even the things that used to have color. So no, I can’t really say I enjoy much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, not really. I mean, I keep trying the things I used to like—my watercolor books, mostly. I’ve got this one with old seaside paintings, and I used to sit with it after work sometimes… back when the library still had that quiet hour before closing, you know.

Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it. I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. Lately I mostly just sit at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of… faded. Like the color’s gone out of things. Even opening those books makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three months now, most days. It sort of crept in around the time I started getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through, old forms and stacks of notes, and I kept thinking I should be feeling something different about it, but mostly I just felt foggy and low. So, yeah… around three months.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. There was one with this little sketch of a bridge tucked in the back once, and I remember wondering who had owned it before me… but lately I just end up sitting at the kitchen table with them there, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. My mother was very strict about that, actually—she’d save even the smallest bit of broth for the next day. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head’s been… foggy, I guess is the word. I work around cataloguing notes, old ones mostly, and I used to be able to keep my place just fine—like, you know, the little numbers and entries, they have a rhythm to them after a while. Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it. I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. Lately I mostly just sit at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of… faded. Like the color’s gone out of things. Even opening those books makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three months now, most days. It sort of crept in around the time I started getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through, old forms and stacks of notes, and I kept thinking I should be feeling something different about it, but mostly I just felt foggy and low. So, yeah… around three months.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. There was one with this little sketch of a bridge tucked in the back once, and I remember wondering who had owned it before me… but lately I just end up sitting at the kitchen table with them there, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes when I’m sitting there and it’s quiet, I think about how strange it is that people leave all these little traces behind—notes in books, old checkout cards, handwriting—and then they’re just gone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really… I don’t have something clear to tell you about that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: My appetite, yes… that’s been different. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t eaten unless I sort of remind myself.\n\nMy wife notices it more than I do, I think. She’ll say, “You barely touched that,” because I leave the rice and soup half-finished. And I never used to waste a meal. My mother was very strict about that, actually—she’d save even the smallest bit of broth for the next day. But lately I just sort of sit there, and the bowl gets cold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not like I’m limping or anything, but my whole body feels slow. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because my head’s been… foggy, I guess is the word. I work around cataloguing notes, old ones mostly, and I used to be able to keep my place just fine—like, you know, the little numbers and entries, they have a rhythm to them after a while. Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it. I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. Lately I mostly just sit at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of… faded. Like the color’s gone out of things. Even opening those books makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three months now, most days. It sort of crept in around the time I started getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through, old forms and stacks of notes, and I kept thinking I should be feeling something different about it, but mostly I just felt foggy and low. So, yeah… around three months.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. There was one with this little sketch of a bridge tucked in the back once, and I remember wondering who had owned it before me… but lately I just end up sitting at the kitchen table with them there, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes when I’m sitting there and it’s quiet, I think about how strange it is that people leave all these little traces behind—notes in books, old checkout cards, handwriting—and then they’re just gone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really… I don’t have something clear to tell you about that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: My appetite, yes… that’s been different. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t eaten unless I sort of remind myself.\n\nMy wife notices it more than I do, I think. She’ll say, “You barely touched that,” because I leave the rice and soup half-finished. And I never used to waste a meal. My mother was very strict about that, actually—she’d save even the smallest bit of broth for the next day. But lately I just sort of sit there, and the bowl gets cold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not like I’m limping or anything, but my whole body feels slow. Even walking to the corner store feels like it takes more out of me than it should.\n\nAt work, too, just sorting papers on my desk… I used to do that without thinking, you know, make little piles, straighten things up. Now I sit there looking at them, and it feels like my hands take a long time to get started. Funny, my father used to say a cluttered desk meant a cluttered mind, though his garage was a disaster… but yes, I guess I do feel slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I can’t say a number. But I suppose I might have, because I’m eating less. I have to remind myself sometimes, like, “Oh, you haven’t had lunch,” which is odd.\n\nMy wife notices more than I do. She’ll point out that I left half the rice or soup, and I never used to do that. Wasting food was a big thing in our house growing up… you didn’t leave rice in the bowl. So, yes, maybe, but I don’t know exactly how much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently difficult?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty consistently difficult, I’d say. I mean, there are little moments where I get distracted and it doesn’t feel as heavy, but most days… the low mood and that foggy feeling have been there.\n\nIt’s been about three months now, I think. Around when I started getting things ready to leave the library. Which is strange, because you’d think having time away from all those files and carts and little date stamps would feel like a relief, but it hasn’t really worked that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two weeks where you felt even slightly better for at least a couple of days?\n\nPatient: Not really for a couple of days, no. I mean, there might be a half hour where I’m watching the birds out by the fence and I forget myself a little, but mostly… most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nEven my old watercolor books, the ones I used to like looking through—there’s one with these seaside scenes, my aunt had something similar in her hallway years ago—now I open them and instead of feeling interested, I just feel heavy. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a couple of better days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt normal, like your old self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve really felt like my old self. Most days I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nEven those old watercolor books I used to enjoy—there’s one with these soft seaside paintings, I used to look at it for ages—now I open it and it just makes me feel heavy instead of interested. So, no, not normal, really.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I keep thinking maybe I’ll get some comfort from the things I used to like… like those old watercolor books I’ve got. There’s one with these washed-out seaside pictures, and I used to sit with it a long while, noticing little bits of color.\n\nBut lately I just end up at the kitchen table, and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it all. I open the book and instead of enjoying it, it just makes me feel heavy. It’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Instead of feeling interested, I just feel heavy. Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes when I’m sitting there and it’s quiet, I think about how strange it is that people leave all these little traces behind—notes in books, old checkout cards, handwriting—and then they’re just gone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really… I don’t have something clear to tell you about that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: My appetite, yes… that’s been different. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t eaten unless I sort of remind myself.\n\nMy wife notices it more than I do, I think. My mother was very strict about that, actually—she’d save even the smallest bit of broth for the next day. But lately I just sort of sit there, and the bowl gets cold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not like I’m limping or anything, but my whole body feels slow. Now I sit there looking at them, and it feels like my hands take a long time to get started. Funny, my father used to say a cluttered desk meant a cluttered mind, though his garage was a disaster… but yes, I guess I do feel slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I can’t say a number. But I suppose I might have, because I’m eating less. I have to remind myself sometimes, like, “Oh, you haven’t had lunch,” which is odd.\n\nMy wife notices more than I do. I mean, sometimes when I’m sitting there and it’s quiet, I think about how strange it is that people leave all these little traces behind—notes in books, old checkout cards, handwriting—and then they’re just gone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really… I don’t have something clear to tell you about that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: My appetite, yes… that’s been different. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t eaten unless I sort of remind myself.\n\nMy wife notices it more than I do, I think. She’ll say, “You barely touched that,” because I leave the rice and soup half-finished. And I never used to waste a meal. My mother was very strict about that, actually—she’d save even the smallest bit of broth for the next day. But lately I just sort of sit there, and the bowl gets cold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not like I’m limping or anything, but my whole body feels slow. Even walking to the corner store feels like it takes more out of me than it should.\n\nAt work, too, just sorting papers on my desk… I used to do that without thinking, you know, make little piles, straighten things up. Now I sit there looking at them, and it feels like my hands take a long time to get started. Funny, my father used to say a cluttered desk meant a cluttered mind, though his garage was a disaster… but yes, I guess I do feel slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I can’t say a number. But I suppose I might have, because I’m eating less. I have to remind myself sometimes, like, “Oh, you haven’t had lunch,” which is odd.\n\nMy wife notices more than I do. She’ll point out that I left half the rice or soup, and I never used to do that. Wasting food was a big thing in our house growing up… you didn’t leave rice in the bowl. So, yes, maybe, but I don’t know exactly how much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently difficult?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty consistently difficult, I’d say. I mean, there are little moments where I get distracted and it doesn’t feel as heavy, but most days… the low mood and that foggy feeling have been there.\n\nIt’s been about three months now, I think. Around when I started getting things ready to leave the library. Which is strange, because you’d think having time away from all those files and carts and little date stamps would feel like a relief, but it hasn’t really worked that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two weeks where you felt even slightly better for at least a couple of days?\n\nPatient: Not really for a couple of days, no. I mean, there might be a half hour where I’m watching the birds out by the fence and I forget myself a little, but mostly… most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nEven my old watercolor books, the ones I used to like looking through—there’s one with these seaside scenes, my aunt had something similar in her hallway years ago—now I open them and instead of feeling interested, I just feel heavy. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a couple of better days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt normal, like your old self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve really felt like my old self. Most days I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nEven those old watercolor books I used to enjoy—there’s one with these soft seaside paintings, I used to look at it for ages—now I open it and it just makes me feel heavy instead of interested. So, no, not normal, really.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I keep thinking maybe I’ll get some comfort from the things I used to like… like those old watercolor books I’ve got. There’s one with these washed-out seaside pictures, and I used to sit with it a long while, noticing little bits of color.\n\nBut lately I just end up at the kitchen table, and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it all. I open the book and instead of enjoying it, it just makes me feel heavy. So I can’t really say there’s anything I’m looking forward to much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still enjoy or find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: Maybe tiny moments, but not really pleasure. I’ll sit at the kitchen table most days, and I keep thinking I should look at my old watercolor books—there’s one with seaside pictures, and it used to remind me of this little print my aunt had, funny how you remember things like that.\n\nBut lately when I open it, it doesn’t feel nice. It just feels… heavy. Like the color’s gone out of everything, even the things that used to have color. So no, I can’t really say I enjoy much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I keep trying the things I used to like—my watercolor books, mostly. I’ve got this one with old seaside paintings, and I used to sit with it after work sometimes… back when the library still had that quiet hour before closing, you know.\n\nBut lately I just sit at the kitchen table and open it, and it doesn’t catch me the way it used to. It’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Instead of feeling interested, I just feel heavy. So… no, I can’t say I’m enjoying much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I get worn down, and some days feel very long, you know, like when the library used to drag on in the afternoons and the fluorescent lights made everything feel flat.\n\nBut thoughts that life isn’t worth living… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted it out in those words. It’s more that things feel empty, or I sit there and lose track of time. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really put it that way in my mind, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen you say “harming myself,” do you mean like… actually doing something to myself? I’m sorry, I get stuck on the wording sometimes. It reminds me of when patrons at the library would ask for a “book about history,” and I’d have to figure out what kind of history they meant.\n\nI guess I’m not sure I’ve noticed thoughts like that exactly. I just know I’ve been feeling very worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think I can say yes to that. Not in a clear way, anyway.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of stuck in my head, and then I start thinking about small things—like whether I left the mail on the counter, or how the library carts used to squeak if you pushed them too fast. Funny how you remember that kind of thing.\n\nBut wanting to end my life… I don’t know. I haven’t really had it laid out in my mind like that. I just feel worn out, mostly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Completely unable… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I’m still sort of moving around, doing little things.

But I’ve let a lot pile up. Lecture plans, travel notes, even simple household paperwork — things I used to be able to sort into neat stacks and just handle. Better for more than two days… I’m trying to think. Lately most days I’m just sitting at the kitchen table, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything.

Lately I look down and I can’t even remember where I was in the line.\n\nAnd then even simple things, like deciding what to do next, feel harder than they should. I’ll just sit there thinking, “Do I start this stack, do I put that away,” and it’s like my mind won’t move. It’s frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days lately I end up just sitting at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it. I’ve got these old watercolor books I used to like looking through—some of them have those little notes in the margins from whoever owned them before, which is interesting in its own way—but now even opening them just makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: It’s changed, yeah. I mean, I used to get something out of those old watercolor books—just looking at the washes and little margin notes, sometimes wondering who wrote them, you know. Lately I mostly just sit at the kitchen table and everything feels sort of… faded. Like the color’s gone out of things. Even opening those books makes me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’d say… about three months now, most days. It sort of crept in around the time I started getting ready to leave the library. There were all these little things to sort through, old forms and stacks of notes, and I kept thinking I should be feeling something different about it, but mostly I just felt foggy and low. So, yeah… around three months.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not much, honestly. I keep thinking maybe the watercolor books will still do it, because I used to really enjoy them—the paper, the old notes in the margins, all that. There was one with this little sketch of a bridge tucked in the back once, and I remember wondering who had owned it before me… but lately I just end up sitting at the kitchen table with them there, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Even those just make me feel heavy instead of interested.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, sometimes when I’m sitting there and it’s quiet, I think about how strange it is that people leave all these little traces behind—notes in books, old checkout cards, handwriting—and then they’re just gone. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI haven’t really… I don’t have something clear to tell you about that, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant changes in your appetite or sleep patterns lately?\n\nPatient: My appetite, yes… that’s been different. I don’t really get hungry the way I used to. I’ll realize it’s late and I haven’t eaten unless I sort of remind myself.\n\nMy wife notices it more than I do, I think. She’ll say, “You barely touched that,” because I leave the rice and soup half-finished. And I never used to waste a meal. My mother was very strict about that, actually—she’d save even the smallest bit of broth for the next day. But lately I just sort of sit there, and the bowl gets cold.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving more slowly than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not like I’m limping or anything, but my whole body feels slow. Even walking to the corner store feels like it takes more out of me than it should.\n\nAt work, too, just sorting papers on my desk… I used to do that without thinking, you know, make little piles, straighten things up. Now I sit there looking at them, and it feels like my hands take a long time to get started. Funny, my father used to say a cluttered desk meant a cluttered mind, though his garage was a disaster… but yes, I guess I do feel slower.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any weight recently?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been weighing myself, so I can’t say a number. But I suppose I might have, because I’m eating less. I have to remind myself sometimes, like, “Oh, you haven’t had lunch,” which is odd.\n\nMy wife notices more than I do. She’ll point out that I left half the rice or soup, and I never used to do that. Wasting food was a big thing in our house growing up… you didn’t leave rice in the bowl. So, yes, maybe, but I don’t know exactly how much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past month where you felt somewhat better, or has it been consistently difficult?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty consistently difficult, I’d say. I mean, there are little moments where I get distracted and it doesn’t feel as heavy, but most days… the low mood and that foggy feeling have been there.\n\nIt’s been about three months now, I think. Around when I started getting things ready to leave the library. Which is strange, because you’d think having time away from all those files and carts and little date stamps would feel like a relief, but it hasn’t really worked that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two weeks where you felt even slightly better for at least a couple of days?\n\nPatient: Not really for a couple of days, no. I mean, there might be a half hour where I’m watching the birds out by the fence and I forget myself a little, but mostly… most days lately I just sit at the kitchen table and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nEven my old watercolor books, the ones I used to like looking through—there’s one with these seaside scenes, my aunt had something similar in her hallway years ago—now I open them and instead of feeling interested, I just feel heavy. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had a couple of better days.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt normal, like your old self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve really felt like my old self. Most days I end up just sitting at the kitchen table, and it’s like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nEven those old watercolor books I used to enjoy—there’s one with these soft seaside paintings, I used to look at it for ages—now I open it and it just makes me feel heavy instead of interested. So, no, not normal, really.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Not much, no. I keep thinking maybe I’ll get some comfort from the things I used to like… like those old watercolor books I’ve got. There’s one with these washed-out seaside pictures, and I used to sit with it a long while, noticing little bits of color.\n\nBut lately I just end up at the kitchen table, and everything feels sort of drained, like the color’s gone out of it all. I open the book and instead of enjoying it, it just makes me feel heavy. So I can’t really say there’s anything I’m looking forward to much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that you still enjoy or find pleasure in doing?\n\nPatient: Maybe tiny moments, but not really pleasure. I’ll sit at the kitchen table most days, and I keep thinking I should look at my old watercolor books—there’s one with seaside pictures, and it used to remind me of this little print my aunt had, funny how you remember things like that.\n\nBut lately when I open it, it doesn’t feel nice. It just feels… heavy. Like the color’s gone out of everything, even the things that used to have color. So no, I can’t really say I enjoy much.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I keep trying the things I used to like—my watercolor books, mostly. I’ve got this one with old seaside paintings, and I used to sit with it after work sometimes… back when the library still had that quiet hour before closing, you know.\n\nBut lately I just sit at the kitchen table and open it, and it doesn’t catch me the way it used to. It’s like the color’s gone out of everything. Instead of feeling interested, I just feel heavy. So… no, I can’t say I’m enjoying much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I get worn down, and some days feel very long, you know, like when the library used to drag on in the afternoons and the fluorescent lights made everything feel flat.\n\nBut thoughts that life isn’t worth living… I don’t know. I haven’t really sorted it out in those words. It’s more that things feel empty, or I sit there and lose track of time. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I haven’t really put it that way in my mind, if that makes sense.\n\nWhen you say “harming myself,” do you mean like… actually doing something to myself? I’m sorry, I get stuck on the wording sometimes. It reminds me of when patrons at the library would ask for a “book about history,” and I’d have to figure out what kind of history they meant.\n\nI guess I’m not sure I’ve noticed thoughts like that exactly. I just know I’ve been feeling very worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of wanting to end your life?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think I can say yes to that. Not in a clear way, anyway.\n\nIt’s more like I get sort of stuck in my head, and then I start thinking about small things—like whether I left the mail on the counter, or how the library carts used to squeak if you pushed them too fast. Funny how you remember that kind of thing.\n\nBut wanting to end my life… I don’t know. I haven’t really had it laid out in my mind like that. I just feel worn out, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last two weeks where you felt completely unable to take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Completely unable… I don’t know if I’d say completely. I’m still sort of moving around, doing little things.\n\nBut I’ve let a lot pile up. Lecture plans, travel notes, even simple household paperwork — things I used to be able to sort into neat stacks and just handle. Now I sit down and it’s like I can’t organize it reliably. I used to be good at that, especially at the library, keeping forms and cards and schedules straight… there was a satisfaction to it, actually.\n\nNow I look at the papers and they just stay there. So, not unable to take care of myself in every way, but I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in the last month where you felt even slightly better for more than two days?\n\nPatient: Better for more than two days… I’m trying to think. Lately most days I’m just sitting at the kitchen table, and it feels like the color’s gone out of everything.\n\nI used to look through these old watercolor books I have — the kind with soft landscapes and little notes about mixing washes — and usually that would pull me in a bit. Now I open them and they just feel heavy somehow, like even the pictures take effort to look at.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I’ve had more than two days of really feeling better. Not that I can point to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
